    If I play `e5d4`, I'm capturing the pawn on d4.
    Is this the best move?
    In many openings (like the Sicilian), this exchange is common. It puts pressure on White's center.

    This is different from `e5d4`.

    If `c6d4`, then `e4xd4` (Pawn captures knight) or `d1xd4` (Queen captures knight).
    If `e4xd4`, then Black's pawn on e5 is still there, and White's knight on c3 is still there.

    So we have two ways to capture the pawn on d4:
    1. `e5d4`
    2. `c6d4`

    Which is better?
    If `e5d4`, we use our pawn to capture.
    If `c6d4`, we use our knight to capture.

    Generally, it's better to capture with a pawn if it maintains your piece's strength or improves your structure.
    If `e5d4`, then `c3xd4`. Now we have knight vs knight in the center.
    If `c6d4`, then `e4xd4`. Now Black has lost a knight for a pawn. Not good.

    So `e5d4` is much better than `c6d4`.

    `d5c3` removes a knight and allows White to improve a pawn.

    White: Knight on d4.

    No, `c6d4` is definitely there. But as I analyzed, `c6d4` would be `c6xd4`, which is a knight capturing a pawn, and then `e4xd4` would be pawn capturing knight. That's a loss of a knight.

    So `e5d4` is the most logical choice to capture the pawn on d4.

    Wait, let's look at `d5f4` and `d5e3` again.
    Could they be any better?
    `d5f4` attacks g3. If White plays `g3xf4`, Black loses a knight.
    `d5e3` attacks f2. If White plays `f2xe3`, Black loses a knight.

    So `e5d4` is the most solid move.
